\displaystyle{\cal T}_{11}^{(2q)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{G_{F}^{2}}{2}|V_{q_{1}b}|^{2}|V_{q_{3}q_{2}}|^{2}\int d^{4}x\,\int\frac{d^{4}l_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{d^{4}l_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{d^{4}l_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\Bigg\{e^{-i(p_{b}+l_{2}-l_{1}-l_{3})\cdot x}

\displaystyle\times\bar{b}\gamma_{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})\left({\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{1})}(l_{1})+\tilde{\cal S}_{1}^{(q_{1})}(l_{1})\right)\gamma_{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})b

\displaystyle\times{\rm Tr}\Biggl[\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{2})}(l_{2})\Biggr]

\displaystyle+\bar{b}\gamma_{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})\left({\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{1})}(l_{1})+{\cal S}_{1}^{(q_{1})}(l_{1})\right)\gamma_{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})b

\displaystyle\times{\rm Tr}\Biggl[\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})\,{\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{2})}(l_{2})\Biggr]\,e^{i(p_{b}+l_{2}-l_{1}-l_{3})\cdot x}\Bigg\}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{m_{b}^{4}}\right)\,,

(3.4)

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{22}^{(2q)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{G_{F}^{2}}{2}|V_{q_{1}b}|^{2}|V_{q_{3}q_{2}}|^{2}\int d^{4}x\,\int\frac{d^{4}l_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{d^{4}l_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{d^{4}l_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\Bigg\{e^{-i(p_{b}+l_{2}-l_{1}-l_{3})\cdot x}

\displaystyle\times\bar{b}\gamma_{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})\,\,{\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{1})}(l_{1})\,\gamma_{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})b

\displaystyle\times{\rm Tr}\Biggl[\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})\left({\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})+\tilde{\cal S}_{1}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})\right)\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{2})}(l_{2})\Biggr]

\displaystyle+e^{i(p_{b}+l_{2}-l_{1}-l_{3})\cdot x}\,\,\,\bar{b}\gamma_{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{1})}(l_{1})\gamma_{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})b

\displaystyle\times{\rm Tr}\Biggl[\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})\left({\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})+{\cal S}_{1}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})\right)\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{2})}(l_{2})\Biggr]\Bigg\}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{m_{b}^{4}}\right)\,,

(3.5)

and

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{13}^{(2q)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{G_{F}^{2}}{2}|V_{q_{1}b}|^{2}|V_{q_{3}q_{2}}|^{2}\int d^{4}x\int\frac{d^{4}l_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{d^{4}l_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int\frac{d^{4}l_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\Bigg\{\,e^{-i(p_{b}+l_{2}-l_{1}-l_{3})\cdot x}

\displaystyle\times\bar{b}\gamma_{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{1})}(l_{1})\gamma_{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})b

\displaystyle\times\Bigg\{{\rm Tr}\Biggl[\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})\tilde{\cal S}_{1}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{2})}(l_{2})\Biggr]

\displaystyle+{\rm Tr}\Biggl[\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{1}^{(q_{2})}(l_{2})\Biggr]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+e^{i(p_{b}+l_{2}-l_{1}-l_{3})\cdot x}\,\,\bar{b}\gamma_{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{1})}(l_{1})\gamma_{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})b

\displaystyle\times\Bigg\{{\rm Tr}\Biggl[\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{1}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{2})}(l_{2})\Biggr]

\displaystyle+{\rm Tr}\Biggl[\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5}){\cal S}_{0}^{(q_{3})}(l_{3})\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})\tilde{\cal S}_{1}^{(q_{2})}(l_{2})\Biggr]\Bigg\}\Bigg\}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{m_{b}^{4}}\right)\,,

(3.6)

where, owing to the fact that starting with the dimension-three operator
D_{\rho}G_{\mu\nu}
, the translation invariance of the quark-propagator is broken, see Section
1.3
, the second term in Eq. (
3.2
) must be explicitly computed and cannot be derived from symmetry arguments.
Note also that in writing Eq. (
3.6
) we have already taken into account that the contribution of the free-quark propagator alone, vanishes because of the traceless property of the colour matrices, cf. Eq. (
2.20
).

It is worth remarking that the expression of the propagator in Eqs. (
1.121
), (
1.124
) is infrared divergent in the limit of massless quark, cf. Eq. (
1.125
). This point will be further discussed later on, for the moment it is important to stress that by setting the mass of the up, down and strange quarks to zero, the logarithmic divergences would appear in Eqs. (
3.4
)-(
3.6
) from the contribution of
S_{1}^{(u)},S_{1}^{(d)}
and
S_{1}^{(s)}
, starting at order
1/m_{b}^{3}
. In the following, in order to regularise the integrals, we keep the light-quark masses finite and only in the final expressions, once the infrared divergences have been subtracted, we take the limit
m_{q}\to 0
, where
q=u,d,s
. Moreover, the computation is performed in
D=4
space-time dimensions since the imaginary part of the integrals Eqs. (
3.4
)-(
3.6
) is ultraviolet (UV) finite at LO-QCD. Alternatively, Eqs. (
3.4
)-(
3.6
) could be calculated in dimensional regularisation setting in this case
m_{q}=0
from the beginning
[
138
,
139
]
.

The manipulation of Eqs. (
3.4
)-(
3.6
) proceeds in a similar way and can be conveniently performed using e.g. the Mathematica package
FeynCalc

[
156
]
. After integrating over the variables
x^{\mu}
and
l_{3}^{\mu}
, Eqs. (
3.4
)-(
3.6
) reduce to a linear combination of two-point two-loop tensor integrals with one external momentum
p^{\mu}_{b}
and of possible rank
r=1,\ldots,4
, of the type

\int\frac{d^{4}l_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\int\frac{d^{4}l_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{\big\{l_{1}^{\mu},l_{2}^{\mu},l_{1}^{\mu}l_{2}^{\nu},\ldots,l_{1}^{\mu}l_{1}^{\nu}l_{2}^{\rho},\ldots,l_{1}^{\mu}l_{1}^{\nu}l_{1}^{\rho}l_{2}^{\sigma},\ldots\big\}}{\big[l_{1}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{a_{1}}\big[l_{2}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{a_{2}}\big[(l_{1}+l_{2}-p_{b})^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{a_{3}}}\,,

(3.7)

with
a_{i}=1,2,3
.
The tensor structure of the integrals in Eq. (
3.7
) can be simplified using the procedure discussed in Appendix
C
for
D=4
. As a result, each integral of rank-
r
in Eq. (
3.7
), is expressed in terms of a linear combination of tensors of the same rank built from the metric tensor
g^{\mu\nu}
and the external momentum
p_{b}^{\mu}
where the corresponding coefficients represent scalar integrals of the form

\int\frac{d^{4}l_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\int\frac{d^{4}l_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{\Big\{l_{1}^{2},l_{1}\cdot l_{2},l_{1}\cdot p_{b},\ldots\Big\}}{\big[l_{1}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{a_{1}}\big[l_{2}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{a_{2}}\big[(l_{1}+l_{2}-p_{b})^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{a_{3}}}\,.

(3.8)

Note that all the possible scalar products of the three momenta
l_{1}^{\mu},l_{2}^{\mu},p_{b}^{\mu}
appear in the numerator of Eq. (
3.8
). In the next step, we use the Mathematica package
LiteRed

[
157
,
158
]
to perform the reduction of the set of scalar integrals obtained, to a liner combination of master integrals (MIs).
Let us introduce the notation

\hskip-9.95845pt{\cal I}_{n_{1}n_{2}n_{3}}\equiv\int\frac{d^{4}l_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\int\frac{d^{4}l_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{\big[l_{1}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{n_{1}}\big[l_{2}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{n_{2}}\big[(l_{1}+l_{2}-p_{b})^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+i\varepsilon\big]^{n_{3}}}\,,

(3.9)

with
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, then the set of master integrals reads

\Bigg\{{\cal I}_{111},\,{\cal I}_{211},\,{\cal I}_{121},\,{\cal I}_{112},\,{\cal I}_{011},\,{\cal I}_{101},\,{\cal I}_{110}\Bigg\}\,.

(3.10)

The first four integrals in Eq. (
3.10
) correspond to the MIs of the sunrise graph with three different masses
[
159
,
160
]
. From the definition in Eq. (
3.9
), it evidently follows that once the expression of
{\cal I}_{111}
is known, the solution for the remaining three MIs can be obtained by differentiating
{\cal I}_{111}
with respect to the appropriate mass parameter, i.e.

\displaystyle{\cal I}_{211}=\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{1}^{2}}\,{\cal I}_{111}\,,\quad{\cal I}_{121}=\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{2}^{2}}\,{\cal I}_{111}\,,\quad{\cal I}_{112}=\frac{\partial}{\partial m_{3}^{2}}\,{\cal I}_{111}\,.

(3.11)

Furthermore, the last three integrals in Eq. (
3.10
) factorise into the product of two scalar tadpoles, in fact, e.g.
{\cal I}_{101}
, can be rewritten as

{\cal I}_{101}=\int\frac{d^{4}l_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\frac{1}{l_{1}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}+i\varepsilon}\,\int\frac{d^{4}l_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{l_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+i\varepsilon}\,,

(3.12)

by performing the change of variable
l^{\mu}_{1}+l^{\mu}_{2}-p_{b}^{\mu}\to l_{2}^{\mu}
, and similarly for
{\cal I}_{011}
and
{\cal I}_{110}
. However, we immediately point out that these do not contribute to
{\rm Im}{\cal T}^{(2q)}
, since the imaginary part of the product of two tadpoles vanishes, cf. Eq. (
B.8
), namely

{\rm Im}\,{\cal I}_{101}={\rm Im}\,{\cal I}_{110}={\rm Im}\,{\cal I}_{011}=0\,.

(3.13)

From Eq. (
3.11
), (
3.13
), it then follows that in order to compute the imaginary part of the set of integrals in Eq. (
3.8
), it suffices to know the solution of the master integral
{\cal I}_{111}
in the physical decay region
p_{b}^{2}\geq(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})^{2}
, in correspondence of which its integrand develops a discontinuity. Using the result presented in
Ref.
[
161
]
, we obtain that
4
4

4

Note that we set
d=4
in the result of Ref.
[
161
]
.

{\rm Im}\,{\cal I}_{111}=\frac{1}{256\pi^{3}}\!\int\limits_{(m_{2}+m_{3})^{2}}^{(\sqrt{s}-m_{1})^{2}}\!\!dt\,\frac{\sqrt{\lambda(t,m_{2}^{2},m_{3}^{2})\,\lambda(s,t,m_{1}^{2})}}{t\,s}\,,

(3.14)

where
s=p_{b}^{2}
.
However, the integral in Eq. (
3.14
) admits a simple analytical expression if at most two masses are non zero. For three non vanishing masses, its complexity highly increases and the solution involves elliptic functions, see e.g. Refs.
[
162
,
163
,
164
,
165
]
. We emphasise that in the approximation of massless
u,d
and
s
quarks, it is always possible to set at least one mass to zero and to compute all the corresponding master integrals analytically, except in the case of
b\to c\bar{c}s
transition where, as discussed above, we need to keep the mass of the
s
-quark finite in order to regularise the IR divergence originating from

S_{1}^{(s)}
. It follows that for this specific mode, we do not provide an analytical expression for all the corresponding MIs, and our results still require a numerical integration.

The integrals in Eqs. (
3.14
)-(
3.11
), are scalar functions of the external momentum
p_{b}^{\mu}
and depend on the dimensionless parameters
r_{i}=m_{i}^{2}/p_{b}^{2}
. At this point of the computation then, taking into account that
G_{\mu\nu}=-i\left[iD_{\mu},iD_{\nu}\right]
, see Eq. (
17
), together with
D_{\rho}\,G_{\mu\nu}=-\left[iD_{\rho},\left[iD_{\mu},iD_{\nu}\right]\right]
, see Eq. (
19
), the imaginary part of Eqs. (
3.4
)-(
3.5
) can be schematically written in the following form

5
5

5

Note that for brevity spinor and colour indices are not shown.

\displaystyle{\rm Im}{\cal T}_{mn}^{(2q)}

\displaystyle={\cal F}_{mn}(p_{b},r_{i})\,\bar{b}b+{\cal G}_{mn}^{\mu\nu}(p_{b},r_{i})\,\bar{b}(iD_{\mu})(iD_{\nu})b

\displaystyle+{\cal D}_{mn}^{\mu\nu\rho}(p_{b},r_{i})\,\bar{b}(iD_{\mu})(iD_{\nu})(iD_{\rho})b+{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{m_{b}^{4}}\right)\,,

(3.15)

corresponding, respectively, to the sum of the contributions due to the free quark propagator and to the two lowest dimensional corrections to this, namely the ones proportional to the gluon field strength tensor and to its first covariant derivative.
We notice that
{\cal F}_{13}(p_{b},r_{i})=0
,
since the free-quark propagator is colour singlet and the contribution of the colour octet operator
Q_{3}
, cf. Eq. (
2.8
), vanishes. Moreover, another feature of Eq. (
3.15
) is that

{\cal F}_{22}(p_{b},r_{i})={\cal F}_{11}(p_{b},r_{i})\,,

(3.16)

which is the generalisation of the result given in Eq. (
2.21
), to the case of an arbitrary decay mode of the
b
quark. Singling out the colour factor due to
\delta_{ii}=N_{c}
, we can write

{\cal F}_{11}(p_{b},r_{i})=N_{c}\,\tilde{\cal F}_{11}(p_{b},r_{i})\,,

(3.17)

and then from Eq. (
3.1
) we readily obtain that

{\rm Im}{\cal T}^{(2q)}\Big|^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}_{d=3}=\Big(N_{c}\,\big(C_{1}^{2}+C_{2}^{2}\big)+2\,C_{1}C_{2}\Big)\tilde{\cal F}_{11}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}(p_{b},r_{i})\,\bar{b}b\,,

(3.18)

which again generalises the result in Eq. (
2.33
).
Furthermore, Eq. (
3.15
) presents also the important property, already encountered in Section
2.1.2
for the specific case of
b\to c\bar{u}d
transition, that at order
1/m_{b}^{2}
only the expansion of the
q_{2}
quark propagator gives a non vanishing contribution, namely

{\cal G}^{\mu\nu}_{11}(p_{b},r_{i})={\cal G}^{\mu\nu}_{22}(p_{b},r_{i})=0\,,\quad\,{\cal G}^{\mu\nu}_{13}(p_{b},r_{i})\neq 0\,.

(3.19)

This result has relevant numerical consequences, since the coefficient of
C_{1}^{2}
at order
1/m_{b}^{2}
, is strongly suppressed.
So far only power corrections deriving from the expansion of the quark-propagator have been taken into account. The coefficient functions in Eq. (
3.15
) depend on the heavy quark momentum
p_{b}^{\mu}
explicitly and implicitly through the variable
r_{i}
. Introducing the standard parametrisation
p_{b}^{\mu}=m_{b}v^{\mu}+k^{\mu}
, see Section
1.2
, and recalling the definition of the rescaled heavy quark field
b(x)=\exp(-im_{b}v\cdot x)b_{v}(x)
, see Eq. (
1.145
), each term in Eq. (
3.15
) can be further expanded in powers of
1/m_{b}
, resulting in higher dimensional operators with additional covariant derivatives acting on the
b_{v}
field. Specifically, in our case, the expansion must be performed up to order
1/m_{b}^{3}
, hence power corrections to the dimension-six coefficients
{\cal D}_{ab}^{\mu\nu\rho}(p_{b},r_{i})
in Eq. (
3.15
), can be neglected as these would lead to contributions of order
{\cal O}(1/m_{b}^{4})
.
Using the procedure described in Appendix
A
, we then obtain

\displaystyle{\cal F}_{mn}(p_{b},r_{i})\,\bar{b}b

\displaystyle={\cal F}_{mn}(m_{b}v,\rho_{i})\,\bar{b}_{v}b_{v}+{\cal K}_{mn}^{\mu}(m_{b}v,\rho_{i})\,\bar{b}_{v}iD_{\mu}b_{v}

\displaystyle+{\cal G}_{mn}^{\prime\mu\nu}(m_{b}v,\rho_{i})\,\bar{b}_{v}iD_{\mu}iD_{\nu}b_{v}+{\cal D}_{mn}^{\prime\mu\nu\rho}(m_{b}v,\rho_{i})\,\bar{b}_{v}iD_{\mu}iD_{\nu}iD_{\rho}b_{v}+\ldots\,,

(3.20)

{\cal G}^{\mu\nu}_{mn}(p_{b},r_{i})\,\bar{b}iD_{\mu}iD_{\nu}b={\cal G}_{mn}^{\mu\nu}(m_{b}v,\rho_{i})\,\bar{b}_{v}iD_{\mu}iD_{\nu}b_{v}+{\cal D}_{mn}^{\prime\prime\mu\nu\rho}(m_{b}v,\rho_{i})\,\bar{b}_{v}iD_{\mu}iD_{\nu}iD_{\rho}b_{v}+\ldots\,,

(3.21)

and

{\cal D}^{\mu\nu\rho}_{mn}(p_{b},r_{i})\,\bar{b}iD_{\mu}iD_{\nu}iD_{\rho}b={\cal D}^{\mu\nu\rho}_{mn}(m_{b}v,\rho_{i})\,\bar{b}_{v}iD_{\mu}iD_{\nu}iD_{\rho}b_{v}+\ldots\,,

(3.22)

where the ellipsis denote power suppressed contributions of order
{\cal O}(1/m_{b}^{4})
and we have introduced the dimensionless mass parameters
\rho_{i}=m_{i}^{2}/m_{b}^{2}
.
Finally, in order to compute
\Gamma^{(2q)}(B)
, we must evaluate the matrix element of
{\rm Im}{\cal T}^{(2q)}
between external
B
states. This can be conveniently done in the framework of the HQET, in which, the residual mass dependence of the
b_{v}
field and of the
B
meson state, can be systematically extracted, leading to a further expansion in
1/m_{b}
.
A consistent procedure to determine the forward matrix element of operators containing multiple covariant derivatives acting on the heavy quark field and to express them in terms of a minimal set of non perturbative parameters, has been presented in Ref.
[
105
]
. Using their results, we readily arrive at the final expression for
the contribution of two-quark operators to the total decay width of a
B
meson, namely

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(2q)}(B)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}\,\Biggl[\Big(N_{c}C_{1}^{2}+2\,C_{1}C_{2}+N_{c}C_{2}^{2}\Big){\cal C}_{0}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}\left(1-\frac{\mu_{\pi}^{2}(B)}{2m_{b}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+\Big(N_{c}C_{1}^{2}\,\,{\cal C}_{G,11}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+2\,C_{1}C_{2}\,\,{\cal C}_{G,12}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+N_{c}C_{2}^{2}\,\,{\cal C}_{G,22}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}\Big)\frac{\mu_{G}^{2}(B)}{m_{b}^{2}}

\displaystyle+\Big(N_{c}C_{1}^{2}\,\,{\cal C}_{D,11}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+2\,C_{1}C_{2}\,\,{\cal C}_{D,12}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+N_{c}C_{2}^{2}\,\,{\cal C}_{D,22}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}\Big)\frac{\rho_{D}^{3}(B)}{m_{b}^{3}}\Biggr]+{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{m_{b}^{4}}\right)\,,

(3.23)

where we have introduced

\Gamma_{0}=\frac{G_{F}^{2}m_{b}^{5}}{192\,\pi^{3}}\,|V_{q_{1}b}|^{2}|V_{q_{2}q_{3}}|^{2}\,,

(3.24)

and the non perturbative parameters
\mu_{\pi}^{2}(B)
,
\mu_{G}^{2}(B)
and
\rho_{D}^{3}(B)
are defined as in Eqs. (
1.159
), (
1.160
).
In Eq. (
3.23
),
{\cal C}_{0}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
refers to the partonic-level
coefficient, which coincides, up to a factor of
(-1/2)
, with that of the kinetic operator
{\cal O}_{kin}/m_{b}^{2}

6
6

6

This follows from the reparametrisation invariance of the HQE, see e.g. Ref.
[
166
]
.
, cf. Eq. (
1.154
), while
{\cal C}_{G,mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
and
{\cal C}_{D,mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
, respectively describe the contribution of the chromo-magnetic and of the Darwin operators. Note that having adopted a covariant definition for these operators, the coefficient of the spin-orbit operator
{\cal O}_{LS}
, cf. Eq. (
1.156
), is identically zero, see for more details e.g. Refs.
[
105
,
138
,
166
]
.
Moreover, we stress that while
{\cal C}_{0}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
and
{\cal C}_{G,nm}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
are finite functions of at most one dimensionless mass parameter
\rho=m_{c}^{2}/m_{b}^{2}
, and
we list their complete expressions in Appendix
D
, as previously discussed, the coefficients of the Darwin operator still depend on the infrared regulator
m_{q}
, with
q=u,d,s
. These in fact, have the following schematic form

{\cal C}_{D,mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}={\cal R}_{mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+{\cal D}_{mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}\,,

(3.25)

where
{\cal R}_{mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
are finite functions of at most the dimensionless parameter
\rho
, while
{\cal D}_{nm}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
absorb the remaining divergent contributions, namely

{\cal D}_{mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}\sim\log\left(\frac{m_{q}^{2}}{m_{b}^{2}}\right)\,,

(3.26)

and their explicit expression can be found in Appendix
E
.
Eq. (
3.26
) shows that the functions
{\cal C}_{D,nm}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
are logarithmically sensitive to the light quark mass
m_{q}\ll m_{b}
. However, the advantage of constructing the operator product expansion lies in the introduction of a factorisation scale
\mu
, in terms of which the dependence on the hard, i.e.
\mu_{h}
and soft i.e.
\mu_{s}
scales with
m_{b}\geq\mu_{h}\geq\mu
, and
\mu_{s}\leq\mu
, is respectively factorised between short distance coefficients and matrix elements of local operators, see e.g. Ref.
[
71
]
. Eq. (
3.23
) alone, including the effect of two-quark operators only, does not correspond to the complete OPE up to dimension-six because starting at this order also four-quark operators contribute. In fact, the logarithmic infrared divergence in Eq. (
3.26
) reflects the mixing between operators at order
1/m_{b}^{3}
under renormalisation. It follows that by solving the corresponding RGEs, the dependence on the light quark mass in the coefficients in Eq. (
3.25
) can be correctly absorbed in the matrix element of local operators, making then manifest the factorisation between hard and soft scales. This is discussed in detail in the next section.

3.2 Operator-mixing at order
1/m_{b}^{3}

Figure 3.3:
Schematic representation of the OPE at order
1/m_{b}^{3}
. At LO-QCD, the Darwin operator is generated at two-loop, whereas the four-quark operators arise already at one-loop.

To understand the origin of the IR divergences in Eq. (
3.26
), and how these are properly subtracted, we study the structure of Eq. (
1.144
) at order
1/m_{b}^{3}
. Within the HQE, the imaginary part of time ordered product of the double insertion of the effective Hamiltonian is expanded in a series of local operators with new effective couplings. This can be schematically written as
7
7

7

Note that the superscript
(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})
and the subscript
mn
are omitted for the sake of a cleaner notation, however they must be always understood.

\langle{\rm Im}{\cal T}\rangle\,\Big|_{d=6}=c_{\rho_{D}}(\mu_{0})\,\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle(\mu_{0})+\sum\limits_{q=q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}}\,\vec{c}_{4q}^{\,(q)}(\mu_{0})\cdot\langle{\vec{\cal O}_{4q}}^{\,(q)}\rangle(\mu_{0})\,,

(3.27)

where the shorthand
\langle\ldots\rangle
denotes a matrix element between external
B
meson states and the dependence on the scale
\mu_{0}
at which the matrix element of the local dimension-six operators are renormalised, is now explicitly indicated. In Eq. (
3.27
), the Darwin operator
{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}
is defined as in Eq. (
1.157
) and we have introduced a compact notation for the four-quark operators listed in Eqs. (
2.100
)-(
2.103
), namely

\vec{\cal O}_{4q}^{(q)}=\Big({O}_{1}^{(q)},\,{O}_{2}^{(q)},\,\tilde{O}_{1}^{(q)},\,\tilde{O}_{2}^{(q)}\Big)\,.

(3.28)

In the following we want to determine the short distance coefficients
c_{\rho_{D}}(\mu_{0})
and
\vec{c}_{4q}^{\,(q)}(\mu_{0})
.
The OPE leading to Eq. (
3.27
) can be schematically visualised as in Figure
3.3
. It has the peculiarity that the order of the Darwin and of the four-quark operators, in terms of the loop- and
\alpha_{s}
-expansion, does not coincide. Specifically, while the four-quark operators are generated at one-loop at order
\alpha_{s}^{0}
, the Darwin operator arises only at two-loop again at order
\alpha_{s}^{0}
. It follows that the one-loop correction to the four-quark operators, shown in Figure
3.4
, is of the same order in terms of loop- and
\alpha_{s}
-expansion as the coefficient of the Darwin operator and must be included to obtain the complete contribution to
c_{\rho_{D}}(\mu_{0})
at LO-QCD, see e.g. Refs.
[
167
,
168
,
169
]
. We note that operator mixing at zeroth-order in
\alpha_{s}
has been extensively discussed for the
b\to s\gamma
effective Hamiltonian, see e.g. Refs.
[
170
,
171
,
172
]
.

Figure 3.4:
One-loop diagram describing the mixing of four-quark operators with the Darwin operator.

In order to evaluate the diagram in Figure
3.4
for all four operators in Eq. (
3.28
), we start by considering
the one-loop matrix element of the colour-singlet operators in the presence of a soft gluon field
A_{\mu}(x)
, which is obtained by computing
the time ordered product of
{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}
,
j=1,2,
with the interaction part of the QCD Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{QCD}^{int}(x)=\sum_{q}\bar{q}\not{A}(x)q
. This reads
8
8

8

Note that summation over colour indices is understood.

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=\langle i\int d^{4}z\,{\rm T}\Bigg\{\vec{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)},\sum_{q^{\prime}}\,\bar{q}^{\prime}(z)\not{A}(z)q^{\prime}(z)\Bigg\}\rangle+\ldots\,

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{b}_{v}\bar{\Gamma}_{j}\Bigg(\int d^{4}z\,iS_{0}^{(q)}(-z)i\not{A}(z)iS_{0}^{(q)}(z)\Bigg)\Gamma_{j}b_{v}\rangle+\ldots\,,

(3.29)

where the ellipsis denote terms with more than one gluon field,
S_{0}^{(q)}(x-y)
is the free-quark propagator
and for convenience we have introduced the notation

\Gamma_{1}=\gamma_{\mu}(1-\,\gamma_{5})\qquad\Gamma_{2}=(1+\gamma_{5})\,,\qquad\bar{\Gamma}_{j}=\gamma_{0}\Gamma_{j}^{\dagger}\gamma_{0}\,.

(3.30)

We recognise in the term in round brackets of Eq. (
3.29
) the first order correction to the free-quark propagator for vanishing space-time separation, cf. Eq. (
1.77
), namely

\int d^{4}z\,iS_{0}^{(q)}(-z)i\not{A}(z)iS_{0}^{(q)}(z)=\lim_{x\to 0}\,iS^{(q)}_{1}(x,0)\,,

(3.31)

which, in the FS gauge, admits the Fourier representation

\lim_{x\to 0}S^{(q)}_{1}(x,0)=\int\frac{d^{4}l}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,{\cal S}^{(q)}_{1}(l)\,,

(3.32)

with
{\cal S}^{(q)}_{1}(l)
given in Eq. (
1.98
).
The integral in Eq. (
3.32
) has both UV- and IR-divergences in the limit of massless quark
q
. To regularise the former we use dimensional regularisation, setting the number of space-time dimension to
D=4-2\epsilon
, for the latter we must choose the same regularisation scheme applied to the computation of the coefficients in Eq. (
3.25
), hence we keep the mass of the light-quark running into the loop finite. The algebra of gamma matrices in
D
dimensions is computed using the naive dimensional regularisation (NDR) scheme, with

\{\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{\nu}\}=2g_{\mu\nu}\,,\qquad g_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}=D\,,\qquad\{\gamma_{\mu},\gamma_{5}\}=0\,.

(3.33)

Taking into account in the expression for the quark-propagator
{\cal S}^{(q)}_{1}(l)
that
G_{\mu\nu}=-i\left[iD_{\mu},iD_{\nu}\right]
and that
D_{\rho}\,G_{\mu\nu}=-\left[iD_{\rho},\left[iD_{\mu},iD_{\nu}\right]\right]
, Eq. (
3.29
) becomes

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{b}_{v}\bar{\Gamma}_{j}i\int\frac{d^{D}l}{(2\pi)^{D}}\,\Bigg\{-\frac{i}{2}\frac{[D^{\sigma},D^{\tau}]}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{2}}l^{\rho}\gamma^{\mu}\epsilon_{\rho\mu\sigma\tau}

\displaystyle+i\frac{2}{3}\frac{[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},D^{\mu}]]}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{2}}\left(\gamma_{\mu}-l_{\mu}\frac{\not{l}}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)}\right)

\displaystyle-i\frac{2}{3}\,[D_{\nu},[D_{\rho},D_{\mu}]]\frac{l^{\nu}l^{\rho}\gamma^{\mu}}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{3}}

\displaystyle+i\frac{[D_{\nu},[D^{\sigma},D^{\tau}]]}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{3}}l^{\nu}l^{\rho}\gamma^{\mu}\epsilon_{\rho\mu\sigma\tau}\Bigg\}\Gamma_{j}b_{v}\rangle+\ldots\,.

(3.34)

We stress that due to the chiral structure of Eq. (
3.34
), terms in the propagator proportional to an even number of gamma matrices do not contribute and that we have used that
\gamma_{5}\Gamma_{j}=\Gamma_{j}
.
Moreover, the first integral on the r.h.s of Eq. (
3.34
) vanishes, being the integrand an odd function of
l^{\mu}
and the integration domain even.
This result is independent of the colour structure of the operator inserted in the vertex of the diagram in Figure
3.4
and applies also to the matrix element of the remaining colour-octet operators in Eq. (
3.28
). Hence,
the four-quark operators
{\vec{\cal O}}^{(q)}_{4q}
do not mix with the chromo-magnetic operator at order
\alpha_{s}^{0}

9
9

9

Note that mixing with lower-dimensional operators can arise at NLO-QCD, see e.g. Ref.
[
173
]
.
.
Eq. (
3.34
) then simplifies to

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=-\langle\bar{b}_{v}\bar{\Gamma}_{j}\int\frac{d^{D}l}{(2\pi)^{D}}\,\Bigg\{\frac{[D_{\nu},[D^{\sigma},D^{\tau}]]}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{3}}l^{\nu}l^{\rho}\gamma^{\mu}\epsilon_{\rho\mu\sigma\tau}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{3}\frac{[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},D^{\mu}]]}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{2}}\left(\gamma_{\mu}-l_{\mu}\frac{\not{l}}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{3}[D_{\nu},[D_{\rho},D_{\mu}]]\frac{l^{\nu}l^{\rho}\gamma^{\mu}}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{3}}\Bigg\}\Gamma_{j}b_{v}\rangle+\ldots\,.

(3.35)

The dimensional analysis of Eq. (
3.35
) reveals an interesting subtlety. From

[\psi]=\frac{D-1}{2}\,,\qquad[D_{\mu}]=1\,,\qquad[l_{\mu}]=1\,,

(3.36)

where the square brackets denote the dimension in units of mass i.e.
[m]=1
and
\psi
an arbitrary fermion field, see e.g. Ref.
[
38
]
, we obtain that while Eq. (
3.35
) is dimensionally correct, since both sides have the same mass dimension, the integrals in Eq. (
3.35
) have now become dimensionful, namely

\left[\int\frac{d^{4}l}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{2}}\right]=0\quad\to\quad\left[\int\frac{d^{D}l}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{2}}\right]=-2\epsilon\,.

(3.37)

This fictitious dimension is an artefact of the regularisation scheme and does not correspond to any physical parameter. In fact, in this case, the integrals in Eq. (
3.35
) would be expressed in terms of ill defined logarithms with a dimensionful argument, cf. Eqs. (
B.1
), (
B.23
). The origin of this mismatch lies in the different scaling between the Darwin and the four-quark operators with the number of dimensions, see a similar discussion, for the case of
b\to s\gamma
and
b\to sg
, in Ref.
[
172
]
, i.e.

\Big[{\vec{\cal O}}^{(q)}_{4q}\Big]=6-4\epsilon\quad\neq\quad 6-2\epsilon=\Big[{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\Big]\,.

(3.38)

In order to keep the integrals in Eq. (
3.35
) dimensionless, we must introduce a scale factor of
\mu^{2\epsilon}
in the integration measure. Eq. (
3.35
) then becomes

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=-\mu^{-2\epsilon}\langle\bar{b}_{v}\bar{\Gamma}_{j}\mu^{2\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{D}l}{(2\pi)^{D}}\Bigg\{\frac{[D_{\nu},[D^{\sigma},D^{\tau}]]}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{3}}l^{\nu}l^{\rho}\gamma^{\mu}\epsilon_{\rho\mu\sigma\tau}

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{3}\frac{[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},D^{\mu}]]}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{2}}\left(\gamma_{\mu}-l_{\mu}\frac{\not{l}}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{3}[D_{\nu},[D_{\rho},D_{\mu}]]\frac{l^{\nu}l^{\rho}\gamma^{\mu}}{(l^{2}-m_{q}^{2}+i\varepsilon)^{3}}\Bigg\}\,\Gamma_{j}b_{v}\rangle+\ldots\,.

(3.39)

Using the results in Eqs. (
B.21
), (
B.24
), to evaluate the scalar and tensor integrals in Eq. (
3.39
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=-\mu^{-2\epsilon}\langle\bar{b}_{v}\bar{\Gamma}_{j}\,\Bigg\{\frac{1}{4}{\cal I}_{0}(m_{q}^{2})[D_{\nu},[D_{\sigma},D_{\tau}]]\epsilon^{\nu\mu\sigma\tau}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{3}{\cal I}_{0}(m_{q}^{2})[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},D^{\mu}]]\,\Bigg\}\gamma_{\mu}\,\Gamma_{j}\,b_{v}\rangle+\ldots\,,

(3.40)

where
{\cal I}_{0}(m_{q}^{2})
is given in Eq. (
B.23
).
The gamma structure on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
3.40
) reduces to

\displaystyle\gamma_{\nu}\,(1-\gamma_{5})\,\gamma_{\mu}\,\gamma^{\nu}\,(1-\gamma_{5})

\displaystyle=2\,(2-D)\,\gamma_{\mu}\,(1-\gamma_{5})\,,\quad j=1\,,

(3.41)

\displaystyle(1-\gamma_{5})\,\gamma_{\mu}\,(1+\gamma_{5})

\displaystyle=2\,\gamma_{\mu}\,(1+\gamma_{5})\,,\qquad\,\,\,\qquad j=2\,,

(3.42)

and by substituting the expression in Eq. (
B.23
), it follows that

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{j}}{24\pi^{2}}\Bigg[\mu^{-2\epsilon}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\gamma_{E}+\log(4\pi)\right)+\log\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{m_{q}^{2}}\right)+b_{j}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\times\Bigg[\frac{3}{4}\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\nu},[D_{\sigma},D_{\tau}]]\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\sigma}\gamma^{\tau}\big(\gamma_{5}+(-1)^{j}\big)b_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle+i\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},D^{\mu}]]\gamma_{\mu}\big(1+(-1)^{j}\gamma_{5}\big)b_{v}\rangle\,\Bigg]+\ldots\,,

(3.43)

with

a_{1}=2\,,\quad b_{1}=-1\,,\qquad a_{2}=-1\,,\quad b_{2}=0\,.

(3.44)

Note that the tensor decomposition of three gamma matrices Eq. (
7
) has been used to rewrite
i\,\epsilon^{\nu\sigma\tau\mu}\,\gamma_{\mu}
in the second line of Eq. (
3.43
), see also Eq. (
2.54
), and that terms
{\cal O}(\epsilon)
have been already neglected. We emphasise that the presence of the constant terms
b_{j}
in Eq. (
3.43
) depends on the choice of the four-quark operators basis in Eq. (
3.28
). This will be discussed further at the end of this chapter.

In order to simplify the structure of Eq. (
3.43
) and identity the relevant operators, first we take into account that due to parity conservation, matrix elements with an odd number of
\gamma_{5}
vanish, then
we recall that the rescaled heavy quark field
b_{v}(x)
satisfies
(iv\cdot D)b_{v}(x)={\cal O}(1/m_{b})
, cf. Eq. (
1.149
), together with
\not{v}b_{v}(x)=b_{v}(x)+{\cal O}(1/m_{b})
, cf. Eq. (
1.148
). It follows that the two operators in the second and third line of Eq. (
3.43
), respectively give

\displaystyle\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\nu},[D_{\sigma},D_{\tau}]]\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\sigma}\gamma^{\tau}\,b_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Bigg(\langle\bar{b}_{v}\not{v}[D_{\nu},[D_{\sigma},D_{\tau}]]

\displaystyle\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\sigma}\gamma^{\tau}b_{v}\rangle+\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\nu},[D_{\sigma},D_{\tau}]]\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\sigma}\gamma^{\tau}\not{v}b_{v}\rangle\Bigg)+\ldots

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\nu},[D_{\sigma},D_{\tau}]]\Big(\not{v}

\displaystyle\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\sigma}\gamma^{\tau}+\gamma^{\nu}\not{v}\gamma^{\sigma}\gamma^{\tau}+2v^{\tau}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\sigma}-2v^{\sigma}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\tau}\Big)b_{v}\rangle+\ldots

\displaystyle=-i\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\nu},

\displaystyle[D_{\sigma},D_{\tau}]]\Big(v^{\nu}\sigma^{\sigma\tau}+v^{\tau}\sigma^{\nu\sigma}-v^{\sigma}\sigma^{\nu\tau}\Big)\,b_{v}\rangle+\ldots

\displaystyle\qquad={\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{m_{b}}\right)\,,

(3.45)

where in the last step we have taken into account the result in Eq. (
1.67
), which applies up to order
{\cal O}(1/m_{b})
, moreover

\displaystyle\quad i\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},D^{\mu}]]\gamma_{\mu}b_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\Big(\langle\bar{b}_{v}\not{v}[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},

\displaystyle D^{\mu}]]\gamma_{\mu}b_{v}\rangle+\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},D^{\mu}]]\gamma_{\mu}\not{v}b_{v}\rangle\Big)+\ldots

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}

\displaystyle\langle\bar{b}_{v}[D_{\rho},[D^{\rho},D^{\mu}]]\{\not{v},\gamma_{\mu}\}b_{v}\rangle+\ldots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\langle\bar{b}_{v}[iD_{\rho},[iD^{\mu},iD^{\rho}]]v_{\mu}b_{v}\rangle+\ldots

\displaystyle=2\,\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}+{\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{m_{b}}\right)\,,

(3.46)

with

\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}=\frac{1}{2}\,\langle B|\bar{b}_{v}\,[iD_{\rho},[iv\cdot D,iD^{\rho}]]\,b_{v}|B\rangle\,.

(3.47)

Using Eqs. (
3.45
), (
3.46
), from Eq. (
3.43
) we finally obtain

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=\frac{a_{j}}{12\pi^{2}}\,\Bigg[\mu^{-2\epsilon}\,\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\gamma_{E}+\log(4\pi)\right)+\log\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{m_{q}^{2}}\right)+b_{j}\Bigg]\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}+\ldots\,,

(3.48)

and the ellipsis refer to terms of higher order in
\alpha_{s}
and
1/m_{b}
.
We can now consider the one-loop matrix element of the colour octet operators
{\cal O}_{4q,j+2}^{(q)}
,
j=1,2
, namely

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j+2}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=\langle i\int d^{4}z\,{\rm T}\Bigg\{\vec{\cal O}_{4q,j+2}^{(q)},\sum_{q^{\prime}}\bar{q}^{\prime}(z)\not{A}(z)q^{\prime}(z)\Bigg\}\rangle+\ldots

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{b}_{v}^{l}\bar{\Gamma}_{j}t^{a}_{lm}\Bigg(\int d^{4}z\,iS_{0}^{(q)}(-z)i\not{A}^{b}(z)t^{b}_{mr}\,iS_{0}^{(q)}(z)\Bigg)\Gamma_{j}t^{a}_{rs}b^{s}_{v}\rangle+\ldots\,.

(3.49)

where for clarity the colour indices have been explicitly indicated. Taking into account the relations in Eqs. (
9
), (
11
), it is straightforward to simplify the product of three colour matrices on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
3.49
), i.e.

\displaystyle\big(t^{a}\cdot t^{b}\cdot t^{a}\big)_{ls}

\displaystyle=\big(t^{b}\cdot t^{a}\cdot t^{a}\big)_{ls}+if^{abc}\big(t^{c}\cdot t^{a}\big)_{ls}

\displaystyle=C_{F}t^{b}_{ls}-\frac{1}{2}f^{bac}f^{dac}t^{d}_{ls}

\displaystyle=t^{b}_{ls}\left(C_{F}-\frac{C_{A}}{2}\right)=-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}t^{b}_{ls}\,.

(3.50)

Substituting Eq. (
3.50
) into Eq. (
3.49
) and comparing with Eq. (
3.29
) we can readily obtain that

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j+2}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}\,\langle\bar{b}_{v}\bar{\Gamma}_{j}\Bigg(\int d^{4}z\,iS_{0}^{(q)}(-z)i\not{A}(z)iS_{0}^{(q)}(z)\Bigg)\Gamma_{j}b_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}\,\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}+\ldots\,,

(3.51)

and the ellipsis stand for terms of order
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})
and
{\cal O}(1/m_{b})
.
Eq. (
3.51
) shows that up to higher order corrections, to compute the mixing between the four-quark and the Darwin operators, it is sufficient to know only the contribution of the colour singlet operators
\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\rangle_{1-loop}
. The basis in Eq. (
3.28
) is then redundant and the computation would appear simpler if we would had chosen the equivalent basis
10
10

10

We stress that the computation in Ref.
[
137
]
has been performed in this basis.

\vec{\cal O}_{4q}^{(q)\,\prime}=\Big({\cal O}_{1}^{(q)},\,{\cal O}_{2}^{(q)},{\cal O}_{3}^{(q)},\,{\cal O}_{4}^{(q)}\Big)\,,

(3.52)

in terms of the colour-rearranged four-quark operators

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{3}^{(q)}

\displaystyle=\big(\bar{b}_{v}^{l}\gamma_{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})q^{m}\big)\big(\bar{q}^{m}\gamma^{\nu}(1-\gamma_{5})b_{v}^{l}\big)\,,

(3.53)

\displaystyle{\cal O}_{4}^{(q)}

\displaystyle=\big(\bar{b}_{v}^{l}(1-\gamma_{5})q^{m}\big)\big(\bar{q}^{m}(1+\gamma_{5})b_{v}^{l}\big)\,.

(3.54)

In fact, from

{\cal O}_{4q,j+2}^{(q)\,\prime}=\left(2\,{\cal O}_{4q,j+2}^{(q)}+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\,{\cal O}_{4q,j}^{(q)}\right)\,,

(3.55)

it immediately follows that

\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j+2}^{(q)\,\prime}\rangle_{1-loop}={\cal O}\left(\frac{1}{m_{b}}\right)+\,{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,.

(3.56)

However, we will continue the computation using the original basis in Eq. (
3.28
).

The one-loop matrix elements in Eq. (
3.48
), and then also in Eq. (
3.51
), are divergent in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
and need to be renormalised. For the sake of clarity, let us introduce the compact notation for the dimension-six operators in Eq. (
3.27
) and their corresponding coefficients i.e.

\displaystyle\vec{\cal O}=\Big({\cal O}_{\rho_{D}},\sum_{q}\vec{\cal O}_{4q}^{(q)}\Big)\,,\qquad\vec{c}=\Big(c_{\rho_{D}},\sum_{q}\,\vec{c}_{4q}^{\,(q)}\Big)\,

(3.57)

so that the operator renormalisation reads

\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle^{(0)}=\hat{Z}\,\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle\,,

(3.58)

where
\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle^{(0)}
and
\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle
denote respectively the bare and the renormalised matrix elements and the
\hat{Z}
matrix is constructed, by definition, to absorb the divergences of
\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle^{(0)}
. From

\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle^{(0)}=\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle_{tree}+\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle_{1-loop}+\ldots\,,

(3.59)

and Eqs. (
3.48
), (
3.51
), we can read the expression of the
\hat{Z}
matrix and of the renormalised matrix elements
\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle
, in the
\overline{\rm MS}
scheme
[
174
]
and at order
\alpha_{s}^{0}
, namely

\hat{Z}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
Z_{21}&1&0&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
Z_{31}&0&1&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
Z_{41}&0&0&1&0\\[5.69054pt]
Z_{51}&0&0&0&1\\[5.69054pt]
\end{pmatrix}+\,{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.60)

with

\displaystyle Z_{21}=\frac{a_{1}}{12\pi^{2}}\mu^{-2\epsilon}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\gamma_{E}+\log(4\pi)\right)\,,

(3.61)

\displaystyle Z_{31}=\frac{a_{2}}{12\pi^{2}}\mu^{-2\epsilon}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\gamma_{E}+\log(4\pi)\right)\,,

(3.62)

\displaystyle Z_{41}=-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}\,Z_{21}\,,\quad Z_{51}=-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}\,Z_{31}\,,

(3.63)

and

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle(\mu)=\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.64)

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}^{(q)}_{4q,j}\rangle(\mu)=\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j}\rangle_{tree}+\frac{a_{j}}{12\pi^{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{m_{q}^{2}}\right)+b_{j}\right)\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.65)

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}^{(q)}_{4q,j+2}\rangle(\mu)=\langle{\cal O}_{4q,j+2}\rangle_{tree}-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}\,\frac{a_{j}}{12\pi^{2}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{m_{q}^{2}}\right)+b_{j}\right)\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.66)

where
j=1,2
and
a_{j},b_{j}
are given in Eq. (
3.44
). While Eqs. (
3.64
)-(
3.66
) describe how the renormalised matrix elements depend on the renormalisation scale
\mu
, the behaviour under a variation of
\mu
is obtained by requiring that the bare matrix elements
\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle^{(0)}
in Eq. (
3.58
), are scale independent, i.e.

\frac{d}{d\log\mu^{2}}\,\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle^{(0)}=0\,,\quad\Rightarrow\quad\frac{d\,\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle}{d\log\mu^{2}}=-\hat{\gamma}\,\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle\,,

(3.67)

with the anomalous dimension matrix (ADM)
\hat{\gamma}
defined as

\hat{\gamma}=\hat{Z}^{-1}\,\frac{d}{d\log\mu^{2}}\,\hat{Z}\,,

(3.68)

or explicitly, taking into account Eq. (
3.60
)

\hat{\gamma}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
\gamma_{21}&0&0&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
\gamma_{31}&0&0&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
\gamma_{41}&0&0&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
\gamma_{51}&0&0&0&0\\[5.69054pt]
\end{pmatrix}+\,{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.69)

where

\gamma_{21}=-\frac{a_{1}}{12\pi^{2}}\,,\quad\gamma_{31}=-\frac{a_{2}}{12\pi^{2}}\,,\quad\gamma_{41}=-\frac{\gamma_{21}}{2N_{c}}\,,\quad\gamma_{51}=-\frac{\gamma_{31}}{2N_{c}}\,.

(3.70)

The RGEs for the renormalised matrix elements in Eq. (
3.67
) lead to the corresponding ones for the renormalised coefficients
\vec{c}
, since the product

\langle{\rm Im}{\cal T}\rangle\Big|_{d=6}=\vec{c}\cdot\,\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle\,,

(3.71)

must be scale independent. We then obtain the following system of equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\displaystyle{\frac{d\,\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle}{d\log\mu^{2}}}&=-\hat{\gamma}\,\langle\vec{\cal O}\rangle\,,\\[17.07164pt]
\displaystyle{\frac{d\,\vec{c}}{d\log\mu^{2}}}&=\hat{\gamma}^{T}\,\vec{c}\,,\end{array}\right.

(3.72)

which can be easily solved since
\hat{\gamma}
is constant at this order. Integrating from the matching scale
\mu=m_{b}
to
\mu=\mu_{0}
, respectively yields

\displaystyle\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle(\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle(m_{b})+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.73)

\displaystyle\langle\vec{\cal O}^{(q)}_{4q}\rangle(\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=\langle\vec{\cal O}^{(q)}_{4q}\rangle(m_{b})-\vec{\gamma}\,\log\left(\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{m_{b}^{2}}\right)\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle(m_{b})+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.74)

together with

\displaystyle\vec{c}^{\,(q)}_{4q}(\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=\vec{c}^{\,(q)}_{4q}(m_{b})+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.75)

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D}}(\mu_{0})

\displaystyle=c_{\rho_{D}}(m_{b})+\sum_{q}\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{c}^{\,(q)}_{4q}(m_{b})\log\left(\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{m_{b}^{2}}\right)+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.76)

and we have defined
\vec{\gamma}=\big(\gamma_{21},\gamma_{31},\gamma_{41},\gamma_{51}\big)
.
Substituting Eqs. (
3.73
)-(
3.76
) into Eq. (
3.27
) and taking into account Eqs. (
3.64
)-(
3.66
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\langle{\rm Im}{\cal T}\rangle\Big|_{d=6}

\displaystyle=\left[c_{\rho_{D}}(m_{b})+\sum\limits_{q}\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{c}^{\,(q)}_{4q}(m_{b})\log\left(\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{m_{b}^{2}}\right)\right]\,\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{q}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}\left(c_{4q,j}^{\,(q)}(m_{b})-\frac{c_{4q,j+2}^{\,(q)}(m_{b})}{2N_{c}}\right)\frac{a_{j}}{12\pi^{2}}\left[\log\left(\frac{m_{b}^{2}}{m_{q}^{2}}\right)+b_{j}\right]\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{q}\vec{c}_{4q}^{\,(q)}(m_{b})\cdot\,\left[\langle{\vec{\cal O}_{4q}}^{\,(q)}\rangle_{tree}-\vec{\gamma}\,\log\left(\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{m_{b}^{2}}\right)\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle_{tree}\right]+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,,

(3.77)

in which the dependence on the renormalisation scale
\mu_{0}
cancels, consistently with a calculation of order
\alpha_{s}^{0}
. Dropping for simplicity the the suffix
tree
, we then obtain

\displaystyle\langle{\rm Im}{\cal T}\rangle\Big|_{d=6}

\displaystyle=c_{\rho_{D}}(m_{b})\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle+\sum\limits_{q}\vec{c}_{4q}^{\,(q)}(m_{b})\cdot\langle{\vec{\cal O}_{4q}}^{\,(q)}\rangle

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{q}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}\left(c_{4q,j}^{\,(q)}(m_{b})-\frac{c_{4q,j+2}^{\,(q)}(m_{b})}{2N_{c}}\right)\frac{a_{j}}{12\pi^{2}}\left[\log\left(\frac{m_{b}^{2}}{m_{q}^{2}}\right)+b_{j}\right]\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle+{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})\,.

(3.78)

The l.h.s. of Eq. (
3.78
) has been computed in Section
2.2.1
and in Section
3.1
and schematically reads

\langle{\rm Im}{\cal T}\rangle\Big|_{d=6}={\cal C}_{D}\langle{\cal O}_{\rho_{D}}\rangle+\vec{\cal C}_{\rm WE}\cdot\langle\vec{\cal O}^{(q_{3})}_{4q}\rangle+\vec{\cal C}_{\rm WA}\cdot\langle\vec{\cal O}^{(q_{1})}_{4q}\rangle+\vec{\cal C}_{\rm PI}\cdot\langle\vec{\cal O}^{(q_{2})}_{4q}\rangle\,,

(3.79)

where the coefficients
{\cal C}_{D}
have the divergent behaviour shown in Eq. (
3.26
). Equating the respective r.h.s. of Eqs. (
3.78
), (
3.79
), we can finally read the expression of
\vec{c}^{\,(q)}_{4q}
and
c_{\rho_{D}}
at the matching scale
m_{b}
, namely

\displaystyle\vec{c}^{\,(q)}_{4q}(m_{b})

\displaystyle=\vec{\cal C}_{\rm WE}\,\delta^{qq_{3}}+\vec{\cal C}_{\rm WA}\,\delta^{qq_{1}}+\vec{\cal C}_{\rm PI}\,\delta^{qq_{2}}\,,

(3.80)

and

c_{\rho_{D}}(m_{b})={\cal C}_{D}\,-\sum\limits_{q}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}\left(c_{4q,j}^{\,(q)}(m_{b})-\frac{1}{2N_{c}}c_{4q,j+2}^{\,(q)}(m_{b})\right)\frac{a_{j}}{12\pi^{2}}\left[\log\left(\frac{m_{b}^{2}}{m_{q}^{2}}\right)+b_{j}\right]\,.

(3.81)

It is straightforward to check, by taking into account the results for the coefficients of the four-quark operators computed in Section
2.2.1
, together with the expressions of the divergent functions listed in Appendix
E
, that all the dependence on
\log\left(m_{q}^{2}/m_{b}^{2}\right)
exactly cancels on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
3.81
), leaving the final coefficient of the Darwin operator
c_{\rho_{D}}
, free of any IR divergences. Notice though that as consequence of the operator mixing,
{\cal C}_{D}
and
c_{\rho_{D}}
differ also by a finite contribution due to the presence of the constants
b_{j}
.
The complete expressions for the coefficients
c_{\rho_{D}}
are presented in the next section.

3.3 Analytical expressions for the coefficients of the Darwin operator

The final expression for the contribution of the Darwin operator to the inclusive decay width of a
B
meson, induced by the flavour-changing transition

b\to q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3}
, with
q_{1},q_{2}=\{u,c\}
and
q_{3}=\{d,s\}
, is presented in the following form
11
11

11

Note that the coefficients originally presented in Ref.
[
137
]
, still depend on the renormalisation scale
\mu_{0}
since the running of the matrix element of the corresponding four-quark operators was not explicitly included.

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\rho_{D}}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}(B)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}\,\Bigl(N_{c}C_{1}^{2}\,{c}_{\rho_{D},\,11}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+2\,C_{1}C_{2}\,{c}_{\rho_{D},\,12}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+N_{c}C_{2}^{2}\,{c}_{\rho_{D},\,22}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}\Bigr)\,\frac{\rho_{D}^{3}(B)}{m_{b}^{3}},

(3.82)

where the non perturbative parameter
\rho_{D}(B)
is defined as in Eq. (
1.160
),
\Gamma_{0}
is given in Eq. (
3.24
) and the coefficients
c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
respectively read

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},11}^{(u\bar{u}d)}=6\,,\qquad c_{\rho_{D},12}^{(u\bar{u}d)}=-\frac{34}{3}\,,\qquad{c}_{\rho_{D},22}^{(u\bar{u}d)}=6\,,

(3.83)

for the
b\to u\bar{u}d
mode,

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},11}^{(u\bar{c}s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}(1-\rho)\biggl[9+11\rho-12\rho^{2}\log(\rho)-\,24\left(1-\rho^{2}\right)\log(1-\rho)-25\rho^{2}+5\rho^{3}\biggl]\,,

(3.84)

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},12}^{(u\bar{c}s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\biggl[-41-12\left(2+5\rho+2\rho^{2}-2\rho^{3}\right)\log(\rho)-\,48(1-\rho)^{2}(1+\rho)\log(1-\rho)

\displaystyle\ +26\rho-18\rho^{2}+38\rho^{3}-5\rho^{4}\biggl]\,,

(3.85)

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},22}^{(u\bar{c}s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}(1-\rho)\biggl[9+11\rho-12\rho^{2}\log(\rho)-\,24\left(1-\rho^{2}\right)\log(1-\rho)-25\rho^{2}+5\rho^{3}\biggr]\,,

(3.86)

in the case of
b\to u\bar{c}s
transition,

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},11}^{(c\bar{u}d)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\biggl[17+12\log(\rho)-16\rho-12\rho^{2}+16\rho^{3}-5\rho^{4}\biggr],

(3.87)

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},12}^{(c\bar{u}d)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\biggl[-9+12\left(1-3\rho^{2}+\rho^{3}\right)\log(\rho)

\displaystyle+\,24(1-\rho)^{3}\log(1-\rho)+50\rho-90\rho^{2}+54\rho^{3}-5\rho^{4}\biggr]\,,

(3.88)

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},22}^{(c\bar{u}d)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}(1-\rho)\biggl[9+11\rho-12\rho^{2}\log(\rho)

\displaystyle-\,24\left(1-\rho^{2}\right)\log(1-\rho)-25\rho^{2}+5\rho^{3}\biggr]\,,

(3.89)

for the
b\to u\bar{c}s
mode and finally

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},11}^{(c\bar{c}s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\Biggl[\sqrt{1-4\rho}\left(17+8\rho-22\rho^{2}-60\rho^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\,12\left(1-\rho-2\rho^{2}+2\rho^{3}+10\rho^{4}\right)\log\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}{1-\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}\right)\Biggr],

(3.90)

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},12}^{(c\bar{c}s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\Biggl[\sqrt{1-4\rho}\left(-45+46\rho-106\rho^{2}-60\rho^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\,12\left(1+4\rho^{2}-16\rho^{3}-10\rho^{4}\right)\log\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}{1-\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle+\,8\,\left[{\cal M}_{112}(\rho,\eta)-\sqrt{1-4\rho}\,\log(\eta)\right]\Bigr|_{\eta\to 0}\,,

(3.91)

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},22}^{(c\bar{c}s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\Biggl[\sqrt{1-4\rho}\left(-3+22\rho-34\rho^{2}-60\rho^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\,24\rho\left(1+\rho+2\rho^{2}+5\rho^{3}\right)\log\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}{1-\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle+\,8\,\left[{\cal M}_{112}(\rho,\eta)-\sqrt{1-4\rho}\,\log(\eta)\right]\Bigr|_{\eta\to 0}\,,

(3.92)

in the case of
b\to c\bar{c}s
decay.

Figure 3.5:
Relative effect of the Darwin operator with respect to the dimension-three term for the
b\to c\bar{u}d
(top left),

b\to u\bar{u}d
(top right),
b\to c\bar{c}s
(bottom left), and
b\to u\bar{c}s
(bottom right) transitions. For each mode, the green dotted, the solid cyan and the dotted-dashed orange lines correspond respectively to the
Q_{1}\otimes Q_{1}
,
Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2}
and
Q_{2}\otimes Q_{2}
contributions. The reference values
m_{b}=4.5
GeV and
\rho_{D}^{3}=0.2
GeV
3
have been used and the dashed vertical line indicates the approximate
value
\rho=0.05
in the
\overline{{\rm MS}}
scheme.

In Eqs. (
3.84
)-(
3.92
) the dimensionless mass parameter is
\rho=m_{c}^{2}/m_{b}^{2}
, moreover the master integral
{\cal M}_{112}
in Eqs. (
3.91
), (
3.92
) is defined as

{\cal M}_{112}(\rho,\eta)=-\!\!\!\int\limits_{(\sqrt{\rho}+\sqrt{\eta})^{2}}^{(1-\sqrt{\rho})^{2}}\!\!\!dt\,\frac{\left(t^{2}-2(1+\rho)t+(1-\rho)^{2}\right)(t-\eta+\rho)}{t\sqrt{\left(t^{2}-2(1+\rho)t+(1-\rho)^{2}\right)\left(t^{2}-2t(\eta+\rho)+(\eta-\rho)^{2}\right)}}\,,

(3.93)

with
\eta=m_{q}^{2}/m_{b}^{2}
. We emphasise however, that the analytical expression for the limits in Eqs. (
3.91
), (
3.92
), has been derived in Ref.
[
138
]
, namely

\displaystyle\left[{\cal M}_{112}(\rho,\eta)-\sqrt{1^{\!\!\!\phantom{1}}-4\rho}\,\log(\eta)\right]\Bigr|_{\eta\to 0}

\displaystyle=2(1-\rho)\log\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4\rho}}{1-\sqrt{1-4\rho}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{1-4\rho}\,\left[1+2\log(\rho)-4\log\left(\sqrt{1-4\rho}\right)\right]\,,

(3.94)

in terms of which Eqs. (
3.91
), (
3.92
), can be simplified as

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},12}^{(c\bar{c}s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\Biggl[\sqrt{1-4\rho}\left(-33+24\log(\rho)-24\log(1-4\rho)+46\rho-106\rho^{2}-60\rho^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\,12\left(3-2\rho+4\rho^{2}-16\rho^{3}-10\rho^{4}\right)\log\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}{1-\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}\right)\Biggr],

(3.95)

\displaystyle c_{\rho_{D},22}^{(c\bar{c}s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\Biggl[\sqrt{1-4\rho}\left(9+24\log(\rho)-24\log(1-4\rho)+22\rho-34\rho^{2}-60\rho^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\,24\left(1-2\rho-\rho^{2}-2\rho^{3}-5\rho^{4}\right)\log\left(\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}{1-\sqrt{1-4\rho^{\phantom{\!1}}}}\right)\Biggr].

(3.96)

Finally, we stress that for
m_{d}=m_{s}=0
, the following relations hold, i.e.

c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(c\bar{u}d)}=c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(c\bar{u}s)}\,,\qquad c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(c\bar{c}s)}=c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(c\bar{c}d)}\,,\qquad c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(u\bar{u}d)}=c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(u\bar{u}s)}\,,\qquad c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(u\bar{c}s)}=c_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(u\bar{c}d)}\,.

(3.97)

The relative effect of the Darwin operator with respect to the corresponding partonic-level contribution
{\cal C}_{0}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
is given by

\Delta_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}=\frac{{c}_{\rho_{D},mn}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}}{{\cal C}_{0}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}}\,\frac{\rho_{D}^{3}}{m_{b}^{3}}\,.

(3.98)

In Figure
3.5
, the dependence of the functions in Eq. (
3.98
) on the dimensionless mass parameter
\rho
, is plotted for all the three colour structures and the four modes,
using for reference the values
m_{b}=4.5
GeV and
\rho_{D}^{3}=0.2
GeV
3
. Furthermore, Figure
3.6

shows the total relative contribution for each mode, namely

\Delta_{\rho_{D}}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}=\frac{3\,C_{1}^{2}\,{c}_{\rho_{D},\,11}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+2\,C_{1}C_{2}\,{c}_{\rho_{D},\,12}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}+3\,C_{2}^{2}\,{c}_{\rho_{D},\,22}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}}{\Big(3\,C_{1}^{2}+2\,C_{1}C_{2}+3\,C_{2}^{2}\Big)\,{\cal C}_{0}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}}\,\frac{\rho_{D}^{3}}{m_{b}^{3}}\,,

(3.99)

indicating that the Darwin operator can lead to sizeable corrections to the
b\to q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3}
decay width, of the order of
1-7\,\%
(for
\rho=0.05
).

Figure 3.6:
Relative size of the Darwin term compared to the partonic-level contribution respectively for the
b\to u\bar{u}d
(dashed orange),
b\to c\bar{u}d
(solid purple),
b\to u\bar{c}s
(dotted green)
and
b\to c\bar{c}s
(dot-dashed cyan) modes. The reference values
m_{b}=4.5
GeV and
\rho_{D}^{3}=0.2
GeV
3
have been used and the dashed vertical line indicates the approximate value
\rho=0.05

in the
\overline{{\rm MS}}
scheme.

A final comment about the numerical effect of the constant terms
b_{j}
in Eq. (
3.81
)
for the coefficients
{c}_{\rho_{D},mm}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}q_{3})}
.
We have already noticed that their values depend on the choice of the four-quark operators basis.
Consider as an example the coefficient
{c}_{\rho_{D},12}^{(c\bar{u}d)}
. In our basis given by Eq. (
3.28
),

{c}_{\rho_{D},12}^{(c\bar{u}d)}=-29.0
, for the reference value
\rho=0.05
.
Had we chosen the same basis as done in Ref.
[
138
]
, namely with
{O}_{1}^{(u)}
and
O_{2}^{(u)}
, replaced by

{\cal O}_{1}^{(u)}=(\bar{b}_{v}^{\,i}\Gamma^{\sigma}\gamma^{\mu}\Gamma^{\rho}u^{i})(\bar{u}^{j}\Gamma_{\sigma}\gamma_{\mu}\Gamma_{\rho}b_{v}^{\,j}),\qquad{\cal O}_{2}^{(u)}=(\bar{b}_{v}^{\,i}\Gamma^{\sigma}\not{v}\Gamma^{\rho}u^{i})(\bar{u}^{j}\Gamma_{\sigma}\not{v}\Gamma_{\rho}b_{v}^{\,j}),

(3.100)

we would have obtained in Eq. (
3.44
), that

a_{1}=8,\,

b_{1}=-5/4
and

a_{2}=2,\,

b_{2}=-3/2
,
leading to

{c}_{\rho_{D},12}^{(c\bar{u}d)}=-24.0
, for the same value of
\rho
. This shift of
\sim 17\%
must be compensated, up to corrections of higher orders, by the different value of the matrix element of the operators defined in these two bases.

Chapter 4   Phenomenology of Lifetime and Mixing

In this last chapter, we consider two phenomenological applications of the HQE in the charm sector, specifically, the study of the lifetime of charmed mesons and of neutral
D
-meson mixing. In light of the large amount of current and future charm data collected
by LHCb
[
175
]
, BESIII
[
176
]
, and Belle-II
[
177
]
, an improvement of the theoretical understanding of charm physics, see Refs.
[
178
,
179
,
180
,
181
,
34
]
for a comprehensive introduction to the subject, is crucial to fully exploit
the significant experimental progress in this field.
The recent discovery by the LHCb collaboration

[
182
]
of direct CP violation in the charm sector, specifically in the non-leptonic decays
D^{0}\to\pi^{+}+\pi^{-}
and
D^{0}\to K^{+}+K^{-}
, provides one example of this necessity, since after its announcement,
both SM and BSM interpretations of the measurement have been proposed, see Refs.
[
183
,
184
,
185
,
186
]
for the former, and Refs.
[
187
,
188
]
, partly based on the calculation of Ref.
[
189
]
, for the latter
1
1

1

A summary of references investigating a previous claim for evidence of CP violation can be found in Ref.
[
190
]
.
.
Exclusive non-leptonic decays of charm hadrons and even of
b
-hadrons are among the
most challenging observables in quark flavour physics from a theoretical point of view. In the following, we will instead focus on the study of inclusive quantities like the total decay width, for which the HQE provides a systematic theoretical framework, see Section
1.4
.
However,
due to the size of its mass, the charm quark sits at the boundary between the heavy- and light-quark region, making the applicability of the HQE a priory questionable. This is clearly signalled by the fact that contrary to the
b
-sector, lifetime ratios of charmed hadrons can significantly differ from one, which represents the naive expectation in the heavy quark limit. Specifically, lifetimes of charmed hadrons are experimentally determined very precisely

[
191
]
and also inclusive semileptonic branching fractions have been measured
[
191
]
, with a recent update for the
D_{s}
-meson released by the BESIII Collaboration
[
192
]

2
2

2

New results from Belle II have recently been published
[
193
]
:
\tau(D^{0})=410.5\pm 1.1\pm 0.8
fs,
\tau(D^{+})=1030.4\pm 4.7\pm 3.1
fs.

.
A summary of the current experimental status for the lightest
D
-mesons, is shown in Table
4.1
.

[

Finally, a long-standing puzzle in charm physics, is the theoretical description of mixing of neutral

[

,

,

]
. Charm-mixing is experimentally well established and the HFLAV
[

]
average
of Refs.
[

,

,

,

,

,

,

,

,

,
225
,

,

,

,

,

,

]

3

Performed in the case of allowed CP violation.
reads

x=\frac{\Delta M_{D}}{\Gamma_{D^{0}}}=(0.409^{+0.048}_{-0.049})\%\,\,,\quad y=\frac{\Delta\Gamma_{D}}{2\Gamma_{D^{0}}}=0.615^{+0.056}_{-0.055}\%\,,

(4.1)

where
\Delta M_{D}
is the mass difference of the neutral
D^{0}
mesons mass eigenstates
and
\Delta\Gamma_{D}
the corresponding decay rate difference. However, the theoretical predictions for
x
and

cover a vast range of values, which spread over
several orders of magnitude, see e.g. Refs.
[
232
,
233
]
.
Future measurements will not only increase the precision of
x
and
y
, but also provide stronger bounds or even evidence for CP violation in mixing
[
234
]
.
It is clear that having a reliable range of potential SM predictions is necessary in order to benefit from these experimental improvements.

In Section
4.1
we discuss the study of the total decay width of charmed mesons and obtain theoretical predictions for the lifetimes of the
D^{0}
,
D^{+}
, and
D_{s}^{+}
mesons and their ratios, as well as for the semileptonic branching fractions
{\rm Br}(D\to Xe^{+}\nu_{e})
, and their ratios.
Furthermore, in Section
4.2
we present a possible solution for the discrepancy between previous HQE determinations of
D
-mixing with data. The content of this chapter closely follows the one of Ref.
[
140
]
and Ref.
[
235
]
.

4.1 Theoretical study of the total decay width of charmed mesons

In the present section, we analyse the structure of the HQE in the charm sector, to try to shed further light
into the question, whether the expansion parameters

\alpha_{s}(m_{c})
, and
\Lambda_{\rm QCD}/m_{c}
, are small
enough in order to ensure meaningful theoretical predictions for the observables listed in Table
4.1
.
The Particle Data Group
[
191
]
quotes, respectively
for the pole and the
\overline{\rm MS}
mass of the charm
quark, the values

m_{c}^{\rm Pole}=(1.67\pm 0.07)\;{\rm GeV}\;,\hskip 28.45274pt\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})=(1.27\pm 0.02)\;{\rm GeV}\,,

(4.2)

while the dependence of
\alpha_{s}
on both
the charm scale and the loop order, obtained using the
RunDec package
[
236
]
, is shown in
Table
4.2
. We emphasise that in our numerical analysis we use
the five-loop running result.

\alpha_{s}(m_{c})

m_{c}=1.67\,{\rm GeV}

m_{c}=1.48\,{\rm GeV}

m_{c}=1.27\,{\rm GeV}

two-loop

0.322

0.346

0.373

five-loop

0.329

0.356

0.387

Table 4.2:
Numerical values of the strong coupling

\alpha_{s}
evaluated at different scales and loop
order, obtained using the RunDec
package
[
236
]
.

The relation between the pole and
\overline{\rm MS}
mass schemes, up to third order in the strong coupling, reads
[
237
,
238
,
239
]

\displaystyle m_{c}^{\rm Pole}

\displaystyle=\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})\left[1+\frac{4}{3}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\overline{m}_{c})}{\pi}+10.43\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(\overline{m}_{c})}{\pi}\right)^{2}+116.5\left(\frac{\alpha_{s}(\overline{m}_{c})}{\pi}\right)^{3}\right]

\displaystyle=\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})\left[1+0.1642+0.1582+0.2176\right]\,,

(4.3)

where we have used the
five-loop value of
\alpha_{s}
,
at the scale 1.27 GeV. The strong dependence of
\Gamma_{3}
on the charm pole mass, cf. Eq. (
3.24
), leads to different
results according to how higher orders in
Eq. (
4.3
) are treated.
Specifically, by truncating the expansion in
Eq. (
4.3
) at first order in
\alpha_{s}
, and using

\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})=1.27
GeV, we obtain for the pole mass the value

m_{c}^{\rm Pole}=1.479
GeV, which, respectively yields

\left(m_{c}^{\rm Pole}\right)^{5}=\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})^{5}\left[1+0.1642\right]^{5}=2.14\,\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})^{5},

(4.4)

by computing the fifth power of
m_{c}^{\rm Pole}
, and

\left(m_{c}^{\rm Pole}\right)^{5}\approx\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})^{5}\left[1+5\cdot 0.1642\right]=1.82\,\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})^{5}\,,

(4.5)

by further expanding the fifth power up to the first
order in
\alpha_{s}
. Note that the result in Eq. (
4.5
)
is about 15
\%
smaller than the one in
Eq. (
4.4
). Conversely, by including also all the higher order terms shown in
Eq. (
4.3
), gives

\left(m_{c}^{\rm Pole}\right)^{5}=\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})^{5}\left[1+0.1642+0.1582+0.2176\right]^{5}=8.66\,\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})^{5}\,,

(4.6)

which is roughly four times larger than the result in Eq. (
4.4
). In order to deal with this numerical instability, in the following we investigate different scenarios:

1.

Use Eq. (
4.3
) to first order in

\alpha_{s}
, since this is the order at which most of
the Wilson coefficients are known. In this case we fix

m_{c}^{\rm Pole}=1.48
GeV and
\alpha_{s}=0.356
.
A further possibility would be to use as input the pole mass value
from the PDG, i.e.
m_{c}^{\rm Pole}=1.67
GeV. However, in this case, our numerical analysis gives results for the decay rates which are roughly
30\%
larger than the ones obtained in the
1S
scheme, discussed below. Since we expect this enhancement to be compensated by missing NNLO
corrections to the non-leptonic decay rates,
we do not present explicit results for
m_{c}^{\rm Pole}=1.67
GeV.

2.

Express
m_{c}^{\rm Pole}
in terms of the

\overline{\rm MS}
mass
[
174
]

m_{c}^{\rm Pole}=\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})\left[1+\frac{4}{3}\frac{\alpha_{s}(\overline{m}_{c})}{\pi}\right]\,,

(4.7)

using
\overline{m}_{c}(\overline{m}_{c})=1.27
GeV
[
191
]
,
and expand consistently up to order
\alpha_{s}
. Because
of the dependence on the fifth power of the charm quark
mass, in this case,
\Gamma_{3}
receives large
corrections
\sim 5\times(4/3)(\alpha_{s}/\pi)
.

3.

Express
m_{c}^{\rm Pole}
in terms of the kinetic mass
[
104
,
240
]
. The kinetic scheme has been introduced in order to obtain a short distance definition of the heavy quark mass which allows a faster convergence of the perturbative series and which is still valid at small scales
\mu\sim 1
GeV. The relation between the kinetic scheme and the
\overline{\rm MS}
and pole schemes can be found, up to N
3
LO corrections, in Ref.
[
241
]
. At order
\alpha_{s}
we have

m_{c}^{\rm Pole}=m_{c}^{\rm Kin}\left[1+\frac{4\alpha_{s}}{3\pi}\left(\frac{4}{3}\frac{\mu^{\rm cut}}{m_{c}^{\rm Kin}}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\mu^{\rm cut}}{m_{c}^{\rm Kin}}\right)^{2}\right)\right]\,,

(4.8)

where
\mu^{\rm cut}
is the Wilsonian cutoff
separating the perturbative and non perturbative regimes.
In our numerical analysis we set
\mu^{\rm cut}=0.5
GeV, which gives, at NLO-QCD and using as an input
\overline{m}_{c}(3\,{\rm GeV})

[
241
]

m_{c}^{\rm kin}(0.5\,{\rm GeV})=1.363\,{\rm GeV}\,.

(4.9)

4.

Express
m_{c}^{\rm Pole}
in the
1S
-mass scheme, defined as

[
242
,
243
]

m_{c}^{\rm Pole}=\frac{m_{J/\psi}}{2}\left(1+\frac{(\alpha_{s}\,C_{F})^{2}}{8}\right)\,,

(4.10)

where
C_{F}=4/3
, and we use

m_{J/\psi}=3.0969
GeV
[
191
]
,
so that
m_{J/\psi}/2\approx 1.55
GeV. Note that in Eq. (
4.10
), the NLO correction actually starts at order
\alpha_{s}^{2}
, see Ref.
[
242
]
.

4.1.1 Description of the computation

The non-leptonic decay of a charm quark
c\to q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}u
, with
q_{1},q_{2}=\{u,d,s\}
, is described by the following effective Hamiltonian, cf. Section
1.1
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\rm eff}^{\rm NL}

\displaystyle=\frac{G_{F}}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\sum\limits_{q_{1,2}=d,s}\lambda_{q_{1}q_{2}}\Big(C_{1}\,Q_{1}^{q_{1}q_{2}}+C_{2}\,Q_{2}^{q_{1}q_{2}}\Big)-\lambda_{b}\sum\limits_{j=3}^{6}C_{j}Q_{j}\right]+{\rm h.c.},

(4.11)

where we have defined the CKM factors respectively as
\lambda_{q_{1}q_{2}}=V_{cq_{1}}^{*}V_{uq_{2}}

and
\lambda_{b}=V_{cb}^{*}V_{ub}
, and introduced the following notation for the tree-level
\Delta C=1
operators:

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{q_{1}q_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{q}_{1}^{i}\gamma_{\rho}(1-\gamma_{5})c^{i}\right)\,\left(\bar{u}^{j}\gamma^{\rho}(1-\gamma_{5})q_{2}^{j}\right)\,,

(4.12)

\displaystyle Q_{2}^{q_{1}q_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{q}_{1}^{i}\gamma_{\rho}(1-\gamma_{5})c^{j}\right)\,\left(\bar{u}^{j}\gamma^{\rho}(1-\gamma_{5})q_{2}^{i}\right)\,,

(4.13)

while
Q_{j},
with
j=3,\ldots,6
, refer to the penguin operators, which can only arise in the singly Cabibbo
suppressed decays
c\to s\bar{s}u
and
c\to d\bar{d}u
. In Eq. (
4.11
),

C_{i}(\mu_{1})
, with
i=1,\ldots,6
, denote the corresponding Wilson coefficients evaluated at the renormalisation scale
\mu_{1}\sim m_{c}
.
A comparison of their values respectively at NLO-(LO-)QCD and for different choices of
m_{c}
, is shown
in Table
4.3
.

Table 4.3:
Comparison of
the Wilson coefficients
at NLO-QCD (LO-QCD), for different values of
\mu_{1}=m_{c}
.

We see that the Wilson coefficients
C_{j}
, with
j=3,\ldots,6
, are very small and additionally strongly CKM suppressed because of the factor
\lambda_{b}\ll\lambda_{q_{1}q_{2}}
. For these reasons, in the following, the contribution due to the penguin operators in Eq. (
4.11
) is neglected. However, the most general effective Hamiltonian describing all possible
c
-quark decays
is a sum of non-leptonic, semileptonic as well as radiative contributions, namely

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\rm eff}

\displaystyle={\cal H}_{\rm eff}^{\rm NL}+{\cal H}_{\rm eff}^{\rm SL}+{\cal H}_{\rm eff}^{\rm rare}\,,

(4.14)

here,
{\cal H_{\rm eff}^{\rm NL}}
is given in Eq. (
4.11
),

{\cal H}_{\rm eff}^{\rm SL}=\frac{G_{F}}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{q=d,s}\sum_{\ell=e,\mu}V_{cq}^{*}\,Q^{q\ell}+{\rm h.c.}\,,

(4.15)

where we have introduced the semileptonic operator

Q^{q\ell}=\left(\bar{q}\gamma^{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})c\right)\left(\bar{\nu}_{\ell}\gamma_{\mu}(1-\gamma_{5})\ell\right)\,,\qquad\ell=e,\mu\,,

(4.16)

while
{\cal H}_{\rm eff}^{\rm rare}
describes decays like

D\to\pi\ell^{+}\ell^{-}
, whose branching fraction is much smaller than those corresponding to the
tree-level transitions. Hence, in the following, we also neglect the presence of rare decays
and omit to specify further the form of
{\cal H}_{\rm eff}^{\rm rare}
.
Starting from the expression of the effective Hamiltonian in Eq. (
4.14
), the total decay width of the heavy charm mesons
D^{0},D^{+},D_{s}^{+}
, can be computed according to Eq. (
1.144
), where now we need to set
Q=c
. The structure of the HQE is schematically given in Eq. (
1.164
), and its diagrammatic representation can be visualised as in Figure
1.7
. The lowest dimensional contributions, namely those due to two-quark operators up dimension-six, and to four-quark operators up dimension-seven, have been discussed in detail in Chapter
2
and Chapter
3
. Despite having considered explicitly the case of the
B
meson, almost all the expressions obtained, can be used also in the charm sector, taking into account the proper replacements i.e.
b\to c
,
c\to s
, etc.. However, we cannot directly use the coefficients of the Darwin operator listed in Eqs. (
3.83
)-(
3.92
), since by setting
\rho=m_{s}^{2}/m_{c}^{2}
, it is straightforward to see that some of the functions diverge in the limit
m_{s}\to 0
. The presence of IR divergences reflects the fact that now there are further contributions due to mixing of four-quark operators with external
s
-quarks and the Darwin operator, that must be included, whereas for the
b
-system, the corresponding operators with external
c
-quarks did not. This point will be discussed further later on, and we refer to Ref.
[
169
]
for more details.

Following Ref.
[
140
]
, we try to analyse each of the contributions that enter the HQE of a
D
meson, in order to identify the presence of possible cancellations which might affect the charm system. We start from the leading order term
\Gamma_{3}
, cf. Eq. (
1.164
), which, including also NLO-QCD corrections to the short distance coefficients, can be schematically written as

\Gamma_{3}=\Gamma_{0}\,c_{3}=\Gamma_{0}\left[3\,C_{1}^{2}\,{\cal C}_{3,11}+2\,C_{1}C_{2}\,{\cal C}_{3,12}+3\,C_{2}^{2}\,{\cal C}_{3,22}+{\cal C}_{3,\rm SL}\right]\,,

(4.17)

where a summation over all modes is implied and we stress that now
\Gamma_{0}
is defined slightly differently compared to Eq. (
3.24
), i.e.

\Gamma_{0}=\frac{G_{F}^{2}m_{c}^{5}}{192\pi^{3}}|V_{cs}|^{2}\,.

(4.18)

At LO-QCD, the three non-leptonic coefficients
{\cal C}_{3,11}
,
{\cal C}_{3,12}
, and
{\cal C}_{3,22}
, for each of the
c\to q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}u
modes, reduce to
{\cal C}^{(q_{1}\bar{q}_{2}u)}_{0}
, computed in Chapter
3
, cf. Eq. (
3.23
)
4
4

4

Up to the CKM factor
\lambda_{q_{1}q_{2}}^{2}/|V_{cs}|^{2}
.
.
The expressions for the QCD corrections to the non-leptonic coefficients
{\cal C}_{3,11}
and
{\cal C}_{3,22}
,
as well as to
{\cal C}_{3,\rm SL}
, are obtained from Ref.
[
111
]
. In the latter, the computation has been performed
for three arbitrary massive final states of the decaying quark, hence their results can be easily applied to all
c
-quark decay modes, by taking the appropriate mass limits.
For the coefficient
{\cal C}_{3,12}
, we use the results of Ref.
[
114
]
, respectively for
the
c\to s\bar{d}u
,
c\to d\bar{s}u
and
c\to d\bar{d}u
decay channels,
and those of Ref.
[
118
]
in the case of two massive final states, e.g.
c\to s\bar{s}u
.
To compare the size of
c_{3}
between the
b
- and
c
-system, it is interesting to consider the effect of the NLO corrections in the case of dominant CKM non-leptonic and semileptonic modes
5
5

5

For example
b\to c\bar{u}d
,
b\to c\ell\nu_{\ell}
and
c\to s\bar{d}u
,
c\to s\ell\nu_{\ell}
transitions.
, neglecting for simplicity the mass of the final state particles. The result was determined in 1991 in Ref.
[
112
]
, and reads
6
6

6

Note that the factor
|V_{ud}|^{2}\approx 1
has been omitted for simplicity.

\displaystyle c_{3}^{\rm NLO}-c_{3}^{\rm LO}

\displaystyle=8\frac{\alpha_{s}}{4\pi}\left[\underbrace{\left(\frac{25}{4}-\pi^{2}\right)}_{<0}\underbrace{+(C_{1}^{2}+C_{2}^{2})\left(\frac{31}{4}-\pi^{2}\right)}_{<0}\underbrace{-\frac{2}{3}C_{1}C_{2}\left(\frac{7}{4}+\pi^{2}\right)}_{\geq 0}\right]\,.

(4.19)

The first term on the r.h.s. of Eq. (
4.19
), corresponds to the semileptonic mode while the remaining two terms to the non-leptonic one. For the
b
-quark decay, the NLO corrections are negative, while for the charm system, the third term
can dominate over the second one and lead to a positive correction to
c_{3}
. Moreover, there is a sizeable enhancement of the QCD corrections for non-leptonic
b
-quark decays due to finite charm quark mass effects
[
113
,
114
,
115
,
118
]
, whereas the corresponding increase for charm is much less pronounced since
m_{c}^{2}/m_{b}^{2}\approx 0.1\gg m_{s}^{2}/m_{c}^{2}\approx 0.005
.
A comparison of
\Gamma_{3}
, both at LO- and NLO-QCD, for different
c
-quark mass schemes is shown in Table
4.4
.

\Gamma_{3}^{\rm LO}
[ps
-1
]

\Gamma_{3}^{\rm NLO}
[ps
-1
]

GeV

\Gamma_{3}
and LO- and NLO-QCD, using different schemes for the
